```text
[DJ Mauch] Make this podcast:

# THE OVERVIEW

### Episode: Your Next AI Conversation Might Happen In Space

*A short podcast about why some of the biggest companies on Earth are trying to leave it. Approx. 12 minutes.*

-----

[INTRO MUSIC FADES IN, THEN UNDER]

MAYA: Hey, welcome back to *The Overview*. I’m Maya.

SAM: And I’m Sam. Maya, I want to start today with a question. What would you say if I told you that right now, this minute, some of the biggest companies in the world — Google, SpaceX, Amazon, Nvidia — are in a quiet race to build data centers in space?

MAYA: I’d say… you got that off a movie poster.

SAM: *[laughs]* Right? It sounds made up. But it’s real. There are already small computers running in orbit, doing the kind of work that data centers do down here. And the long-term plan is wildly bigger than that. SpaceX has filed paperwork — actual paperwork with regulators — to launch up to a million satellites just for this.

MAYA: A *million*.

SAM: A million.

MAYA: Okay.

SAM: So today’s episode is just: what is this thing? Why is it happening? And what does it have to do with the rest of us walking around down here? You don’t need a tech background. We’re going to keep it normal.

MAYA: Good. Because when you say “data center in space,” I’m picturing the International Space Station with a bunch of laptops duct-taped to the wall.

[BEAT]

SAM: Okay let’s start there. A data center on Earth — when you ask ChatGPT something, or watch Netflix, or send a Gmail — all of that runs through these giant buildings full of computers. They look like warehouses from the outside. Inside, picture a library, except every book is a computer thinking really fast and getting really, really hot.

MAYA: How hot?

SAM: Hot enough that the biggest cost after the electricity to *run* the computers is the electricity to *cool* them. And they drink water like you wouldn’t believe. The newest AI data centers use millions of gallons a day, just to keep the chips from melting.

MAYA: Per day?

SAM: Per day. And that’s starting to be a real problem. Some towns are pushing back. Some power grids — Northern Virginia, Singapore, parts of Europe — they’re tapped out. So the question a few of these companies started asking is: what if we just… didn’t put them on Earth?

MAYA: What if we just sent the warehouse to space.

SAM: Basically, yeah. And when you sit with it, space has some weirdly perfect features for this.

[BEAT]

MAYA: Alright, sell me. Why is space good for computers?

SAM: Three reasons. The first one — the sun never sets up there. If you put your satellite in the right orbit, you can have your solar panels pointed at the sun twenty-four hours a day. No clouds. No night. No weather.

MAYA: Wait. So a solar panel in space is doing the work of —

SAM: Way more than one on a roof. The sunlight is also more intense, because there’s no atmosphere in the way filtering it out. A solar panel in the right orbit produces something like eight to ten times what the same panel does in the best spot on Earth.

MAYA: Okay. Reason two.

SAM: Cooling. Fun fact: space is cold. Deep, dark, almost-absolute-zero cold.

MAYA: Which is the coldest a thing can physically be.

SAM: Right. So in theory you’ve got the world’s biggest freezer sitting right outside your window. You don’t have to pay for it. You don’t have to pipe water through it. It’s just there.

MAYA: That sounds too good. What’s the catch?

SAM: There’s a catch. We’ll come back to it. But the basic idea: if you can get rid of the heat, you can pack way more computing into way less space.

MAYA: And reason three?

SAM: No neighbors. No zoning meetings. No town hall where people are mad you’re building a warehouse next to the elementary school. No farmland getting paved over. No water table getting drained. Up there, nobody cares.

MAYA: Just stars and the occasional dead satellite.

SAM: Exactly.

[BEAT]

MAYA: Okay. Free sunlight, free cooling, nobody to fight with. Why is anyone still building data centers on Earth?

SAM: *[laughs]* Great question. So — the catches. A few of them.
The first is just that getting stuff to space is expensive. It’s getting cheaper because of reusable rockets, but you’re still paying serious money for every pound you lift up there.

MAYA: And computers are heavy.

SAM: Computers are heavy. And the high-end AI chips — the ones everyone wants right now — they were designed to sit in a clean, air-conditioned room, not to take a rocket ride and then survive radiation for ten years. So you have to either toughen them up for space, or build new ones from scratch. Nvidia actually just announced a chip line designed specifically for orbit, earlier this year.

MAYA: Okay. What else?

SAM: Cooling is harder than I made it sound. Yes, space is cold — but space is also a vacuum. And in a vacuum there’s no *air* to carry heat away. On Earth, you blow cold air past a hot chip, the air picks up the heat, you cool the air, you do it again. In space, no air. So instead you have to dump the heat by *radiating* it — basically by glowing infrared into the dark.

MAYA: So you can use space as a freezer, but only if you’ve got a really big radiator.

SAM: Right. And the size of your radiator caps how powerful your computer can be. Engineers are obsessed with this problem.

MAYA: What else?

SAM: When something breaks, you can’t just walk over and fix it. There’s no IT guy in orbit. So the hardware has to either last forever, or be cheap enough you write it off when it dies.

And there’s one more — depending on what the computer is doing, you might need to get the answer back to Earth fast. If a satellite snaps a picture and an AI on board figures out “oh, that’s a forest fire” — great. Beam it down. But if you’re training a giant AI model and the training data is all on Earth, the round trip is slow.

MAYA: So it’s not great for everything.

SAM: Not for everything, not yet. And honestly — there are skeptics. There’s a startup called Varda
```

```text
## Goal

Build a **Podcast Intelligence Pipeline** for DJ Mauch:

**Daily:** Text digest of the most interesting/relevant podcast conversations happening globally — not just subscribed shows but the whole world — delivered to Telegram.

**Weekly:** 15–20 minute two-host conversational audio "podcast of podcasts" — "what's interesting in the podcast world for DJ this week" — using metadata-rank-first, transcribe-finalists approach. Use best available open-source models.

**Architecture:**
- Ring 1: DJ's calibration/reference shows
- Ring 2: High-signal shows DJ may not follow (All-In, Acquired, Marc & Ben, etc.)
- Ring 3: Guest-signal global discovery
- Guest watchlist: ~150+ named people whose podcast appearances should always be surfaced

**Not automated yet.** Calibration run first. Automate only after DJ approval.

---

## Constraints & Preferences

**DJ general preferences:**
- Monitor outputs should be silent-on-OK, actionable-on-alert
- Explicit approval before any recurring monitors/side effects
- Direct CLI execution when access exists; keep building until done or genuinely blocked
- Product design: fast interview/spec before build

**Podcast digest preferences:**
- Daily text from shows DJ actually listens to PLUS global discovery
- Weekly 15–20 min two-host conversational audio digest
- Metadata-rank first; transcribe finalists only
- Prefer best practical open-source models for summaries/audio
- Speaker labels are DIRECTIONS, never read aloud; distinct voices; high-quality TTS preferred

**DJ's intellectual/professional taste (higher-order memory):**
- Wants: market/business shapers, top investors/allocators (VC, PE, hedge funds, banks), CEOs in influential industries, AI leaders, frontier thinkers, framework builders
- Seeks: actionable ideas, changed mental models, new frameworks, personal/professional toolkit additions
- Examples of signal: Gavin Baker, Brian Chesky, Paul Tudor Jones, Stanley Druckenmiller, Ben Horowitz, Hemant Taneja, Michael Pollan, Nick Bostrom
- Skip: news recaps, political commentary, celebrity content, generic VC fluff, second-tier investor promo, shallow origin stories, energy/climate (for this digest), defense/geopolitics
- Selection test: "would this give DJ an action, change a mental model, provide a new framework, or add to the toolkit?"

**Podcast production preferences (audio):**
- Script speaker labels (MAYA/SAM) are stage directions — NEVER read aloud
- Distinct voices per character (not one narrator)
- High-quality TTS: prefer ElevenLabs when available; edge-tts with distinct neural voices when not
- Output should feel like a real two-person conversation
- Include intro/outro music and pauses

**ElevenLabs:** No API key configured in this environment currently. Use edge-tts with distinct voices as fallback.

**X/Twitter access:** `xurl` is installed at `/usr/local/bin/xurl` but returns 401. No X OAuth credentials are configured. Instructions given to DJ to complete `xurl auth` setup manually.
```

```text
### 1. Satya Nadella on Microsoft’s AI platform strategy

**Stratechery / No Priors / Latent Space**

My read: **must listen / digest plus maybe full listen**.

**All-In**

Why it matters:

- AI as both “greatest time to build” and a major portfolio threat.

**Dwarkesh Podcast**

  - labor vs capital share
  - AI as electricity vs AI as social media
  - scarcity after automation

The first metadata scorer is useful but too crude.
```

### assistant

```text
Confirmed. **Yes: we are scoring episodes, not podcasts.**

The correct model is:

- **Shows = source universe / priors**
  - Useful for discovery.
  - Some shows should be watched more closely.
  - Some shows should be heavily filtered.
  - But a show should never automatically be “good” or “bad.”

- **Episodes = actual scoring unit**
  - Every episode gets judged on its own:
    - subject
    - guest
    - timing/relevance
    - likely payoff to you
    - whether it offers an action, framework, market read, operator insight, or mental-model shift

- **Final output = episode recommendations**
  - `must listen`
  - `listen if interested`
  - `digest only`
  - `skip`

And your comments prove the point:

- **Meb Faber / William Goetzmann**: the show may be medium-priority, but *this episode* sounds interesting → should be promoted.
```

```text
{"success": true, "name": "hermes-agent", "description": "Configure, extend, or contribute to Hermes Agent.", "tags": ["hermes", "setup", "configuration", "multi-agent", "spawning", "cli", "gateway", "development"], "related_skills": ["claude-code", "codex", "opencode"], "content": "---\nname: hermes-agent\ndescription: \"Configure, extend, or contribute to Hermes Agent.\"\nversion: 2.1.0\nauthor: Hermes Agent + Teknium\nlicense: MIT\nplatforms: [linux, macos, windows]\nmetadata:\n  hermes:\n    tags: [hermes, setup, configuration, multi-agent, spawning, cli, gateway, development]\n    homepage: https://github.com/NousResearch/hermes-agent\n    related_skills: [claude-code, codex, opencode]\n---\n\n# Hermes Agent\n\nHermes Agent is an open-source AI agent framework by Nous Research that runs in your terminal, messaging platforms, and IDEs. It belongs to the same category as Claude Code (Anthropic), Codex (OpenAI), and OpenClaw — autonomous coding and task-execution agents that use tool calling to interact with your system. Hermes works with any LLM provider (OpenRouter, Anthropic, OpenAI, DeepSeek, local models, and 15+ others) and runs on Linux, macOS, and WSL.\n\nWhat makes Hermes different:\n\n- **Self-improving through skills** — Hermes learns from experience by saving reusable procedures as skills. When it solves a complex problem, discovers a workflow, or gets corrected, it can persist that knowledge as a skill document that loads into future sessions. Skills accumulate over time, making the agent better at your specific tasks and environment.\n- **Persistent memory across sessions** — remembers who you are, your preferences, environment details, and lessons learned. Pluggable memory backends (built-in, Honcho, Mem0, and more) let you choose how memory works.\n- **Multi-platform gateway** — the same agent runs on Telegram, Discord, Slack, WhatsApp, Signal, Matrix, Email, and 10+ other platforms with full tool access, not just chat.\n- **Provider-agnostic** — swap models and providers mid-workflow without changing anything else. Credential pools rotate across multiple API keys automatically.\n- **Profiles** — run multiple independent Hermes instances with isolated configs, sessions, skills, and memory.\n- **Extensible** — plugins, MCP servers, custom tools, webhook triggers, cron scheduling, and the full Python ecosystem.\n\nPeople use Hermes for software development, research, system administration, data analysis, content creation, home automation, and anything else that benefits from an AI agent with persistent context and full system access.\n\n**This skill helps you work with Hermes Agent effectively** — setting it up, configuring features, spawning additional agent instances, troubleshooting issues, finding the right commands and settings, and understanding how the system works when you need to extend or contribute to it.\n\n**Docs:** https://hermes-agent.nousresearch.com/docs/\n\n## Quick Start\n\n``​`bash\n# Install\ncurl -fsSL https://raw.githubusercontent.com/NousResearch/hermes-agent/main/scripts/install.sh | bash\n\n# Interactive chat (default)\nhermes\n\n# Single query\nhermes chat -q \"What is the capital of France?\"\n\n# Setup wizard\nhermes setup\n\n# Change model/provider\nhermes model\n\n# Check health\nhermes doctor\n``​`\n\n---\n\n## CLI Reference\n\n### Global Flags\n\n``​`\nhermes [flags] [command]\n\n  --version, -V             Show version\n  --resume, -r SESSION      Resume session by ID or title\n  --continue, -c [NAME]     Resume by name, or most recent session\n  --worktree, -w            Isolated git worktree mode (parallel agents)\n  --skills, -s SKILL        Preload skills (comma-separate or repeat)\n  --profile, -p NAME        Use a named profile\n  --yolo                    Skip dangerous command approval\n  --pass-session-id         Include session ID in system prompt\n``​`\n\nNo subcommand defaults to `chat`.\n\n### Chat\n\n``​`\nhermes chat [flags]\n  -q, --query TEXT          Single query, non-interactive\n  -m, --model MODEL         Model (e.g. anthropic/claude-sonnet-4)\n  -t, --toolsets LIST       Comma-separated toolsets\n  --provider PROVIDER       Force provider (openrouter, anthropic, nous, etc.)\n  -v, --verbose             Verbose output\n  -Q, --quiet               Suppress banner, spinner, tool previews\n  --checkpoints             Enable filesystem checkpoints (/rollback)\n  --source TAG              Session source tag (default: cli)\n``​`\n\n### Configuration\n\n``​`\nhermes setup [section]      Interactive wizard (model|terminal|gateway|tools|agent)\nhermes model                Interactive model/provider picker\nhermes config               View current config\nhermes config edit          Open config.yaml in $EDITOR\nhermes config set KEY VAL   Set a config value\nhermes config path          Print config.yaml path\nhermes config env-path      Print .env path\nhermes config check         Check for missing/outdated config\nhermes config migrate       Update config with new options\nhermes login [--provider P] OAuth login (nous, openai-codex)\nhermes logout               Clear stored auth\nhermes doctor [--fix]       Check dependencies and config\nhermes status [--all]       Show component status\n``​`\n\n### Tools & Skills\n\n``​`\nhermes tools                Interactive tool enable/disable (curses UI)\nhermes tools list           Show all tools and status\nhermes tools enable NAME    Enable a toolset\nhermes tools disable NAME   Disable a toolset\n\nhermes skills list          List installed skills\nhermes skills search QUERY  Search the skills hub\nhermes skills install ID    Install a skill (ID can be a hub identifier OR a direct https://…/SKILL.md URL; pass --name to override when frontmatter has no name)\nhermes skills inspect ID    Preview without installing\nhermes skills config        Enable/disable skills per platform\nhermes skills check         Check for updates\nhermes skills update        Update outdated skills\nhermes skills unins
```

```text
Totally fair. The simplest mental model:

**Open-source model** can mean two different things:

1. **Hosted open-weight model** — easiest  
   You use a normal API key, like OpenAI/Anthropic, but choose a model such as Qwen, Llama, or DeepSeek-R1.  
   Best for us right now.

2. **Local/self-hosted model** — more “pure” open source  
   You run the model yourself with Ollama/vLLM on a machine with enough RAM/GPU.  
   More setup, more ops, probably not where we should start for this podcast filter.

For this project, I recommend: **OpenRouter + Qwen or Llama open-weight model.**

That gives us the “how good is the filter on an open model?” test without having to run GPUs ourselves.

---

## Recommended setup: OpenRouter

### Step 1: Create an OpenRouter account

Go to:

https://openrouter.ai

Sign in.

### Step 2: Add credits

OpenRouter is pay-as-you-go. You can add a small amount, like $5–$20, just for testing.

### Step 3: Create an API key

Go to:

https://openrouter.ai/settings/keys

Create a new key.

It will look something like:

``​`bash
sk-or-v1-...
``​`

### Step 4: Add it to Hermes

On the Railway/Chief environment, add this env var:

``​`bash
OPENROUTER_API_KEY=REDACTED
``​`

Since your production Hermes is on Railway, the practical way is probably:

1. Open Railway project.
2. Go to the Chief/Hermes service.
3. Go to **Variables**.
4. Add:

``​`bash
OPENROUTER_API_KEY=REDACTED
``​`

5. Redeploy/restart the service.

If you want to test locally in this current environment first, add it to:

``​`bash
/opt/data/.env
``​`

But production should be Railway env vars / secrets.

---

## Step 5: Pick an open model

For the podcast filter, I’d start with one of these:

### Option A — recommended first

``​`text
qwen/qwen3-235b-a22b
``​`

Why:
- strong reasoning
- good at classification/ranking
- good enough for episode-level judgment
- open-weight model family

### Option B — cheaper/faster

``​`text
qwen/qwen3-32b
``​`

Why:
- cheaper
- probably good enough for first-pass filtering
- useful if daily volume grows

### Option C — Llama family

``​`text
meta-llama/llama-3.3-70b-instruct
``​`

Why:
- stable general summarization
- good hosted open-weight baseline

I would start with **Qwen 235B** for calibration, then test whether **Qwen 32B** is good enough for daily runs.

---

## Important distinction

We do **not** necessarily need to change the whole Hermes chat model.

For this podcast system, I’d rather do this:

- Keep Hermes itself as-is for orchestration.
- Make the **podcast ranking/summarization script** call OpenRouter directly with the open model.
- That way the actual filter we’re testing is open-source-model-based, even if I’m helping coordinate from this chat.

This is cleaner because the live daily job can later use the exact same script/model.

---

## What I’ll do once you add the key

After `OPENROUTER_API_KEY` is available, I’ll:

1. Add an OpenRouter client to the podcast script.
2. Configure it to use Qwen/Llama.
3. Re-run the 100 collected episodes through the open model.
4. Produce a new **episode-level calibration markdown**:
   - show
   - episode
   - guest
   - what was said / summary
   - why it matters
   - include/exclude
   - confidence
   - DJ feedback field
5. Compare it against your comments:
   - Meb Faber / Goetzmann should get promoted
   - Mark Pincus should get promoted
   - Vergecast / Nvidia chip war should be considered despite low-priority show
```

### assistant

```text
Yes — and I think you mean “billed,” not “filled.”

Short version: **OpenRouter is prepaid token billing.** You loaded $10, and every time we call Qwen through OpenRouter, OpenRouter subtracts a small amount based on how many tokens we send in and how many tokens the model writes back.

## How open-source model billing works

“Open source model” here means:

> The model weights are open or open-ish, but you are paying someone to host/run the model for you.

So you are not paying Qwen/Alibaba directly in this setup. You are paying **OpenRouter**, which routes your request to a provider running that model.

Billing is usually:

- **Input tokens:** everything we send to the model
  - prompt
  - instructions
  - episode metadata
  - summaries/transcripts
  - scoring rubric

- **Output tokens:** everything the model writes back
  - score
  - summary
  - reasoning/explanation
  - recommendation

For `qwen/qwen3-235b-a22b`, OpenRouter currently shows roughly:

- **Input:** `$0.455 per 1M tokens`
- **Output:** `$1.82 per 1M tokens`

So output is about **4x more expensive** than input, which is normal.

## What your $10 buys

With Qwen 235B, $10 buys approximately:

- **~22 million input tokens** if you only sent prompts
- **~5.5 million output tokens** if it only generated responses
- In a realistic mixed workload, maybe **12-ish million input tokens plus proportional output**, depending how verbose we make it

That is a lot for podcast filtering.

## Compared to OpenAI / Anthropic

Using current ballpark API pricing:

**Qwen 235B via OpenRouter**
- Input: about `$0.455 / 1M`
- Output: about `$1.82 / 1M`

**OpenAI GPT-5.5 via OpenRouter**
- Input: about `$5 / 1M`
- Output: about `$30 / 1M`

**Anthropic Sonnet-class**
- Roughly `$3 / 1M input`
- Roughly `$15 / 1M output`

So Qwen 235B is approximately:

- **~7x cheaper than Sonnet** for this kind of workload
- **~12–14x cheaper than GPT-5.5**
- especially cheaper on output

## What this means for the podcast project

Example costs, using reasonable assumptions:

### 100-episode metadata scoring pass

If each episode uses about 1.5k input tokens and 300 output tokens:

- **Qwen 235B:** about `$0.12`
- **GPT-5.5:** about `$1.65`
- **Sonnet:** about `$0.90`

So your $10 could run something like **80+ full 100-episode scoring passes** at that size.

### Wider daily podcast digest

If a daily run sends broader metadata plus asks for richer summaries:

- **Qwen 235B:** maybe `$0.20–$0.50/day`
- depending on how many episodes and how long the outputs are

If we keep it disciplined, your $10 probably supports:

- **20–50 daily test runs**, or
- several weeks of daily metadata filtering, or
- fewer runs if we start feeding in long transcripts.

### Transcript-heavy mode

Transcripts are where costs can grow.

If we feed 5 full transcripts at ~25k tokens each:

- **Qwen 235B:** still only around `$0.07–$0.15` for that finalist pass, depending output length

So even transcript-finalist mode is not scary with Qwen, as long as we only transcribe/evaluate finalists.

## The thing to watch: output verbosity

The easiest way to waste money is making the model write giant explanations for every episode.

So for the live filter we should use two passes:

### Pass 1 — cheap scoring

For every episode:

``​`json
{
  "score": 0-100,
  "tier": "skip | scan | digest | listen",
  "reason": "one short sentence",
  "confidence": "low | medium | high"
}
``​`

Very short output. Cheap.

### Pass 2 — richer summaries only for candidates

Only for episodes above a threshold:

- summarize what was said
- explain why DJ should care
- recommend listen/digest/skip
- maybe include “what to listen for”

This keeps costs low.

## What you should expect from your $10

For our immediate next step — re-running the 100 collected episodes through Qwen and generating the episode-level calibration markdown — I’d expect cost to be roughly:

**Maybe $0.10–$0.50**, depending how verbose I make the output.

Not dollars and dollars.

For a daily live system, if we run it cleanly:

- **metadata-only daily filter:** likely pennies to tens of cents
- **daily wider digest with summaries:** probably `$0.20–$0.75/day`
- **weekly podcast-of-podcasts script:** probably under `$1`, unless transcript/audio processing gets heavy

TTS/audio generation is separate; OpenRouter only bills the language model calls.

## Important operational note

I checked this running session and I **do not yet see** `OPENROUTER_API_KEY` in the local process or `/opt/data/.env`.

That may be totally fine if you added it to Railway — the current session often won’t see new Railway env vars until the service restarts/redeploys.

So next I’ll need either:

1. Railway service restarted/redeployed so Hermes sees the new env var, or  
2. the key added to `/opt/data/.env` on this machine, if this is the environment we’re testing from.

Once visible, I’ll run a tiny Qwen smoke test first — probably less than a cent — then run the episode-level filter.
```

```text
{"success": true, "name": "podcast-intelligence-digest", "description": "Build, run, calibrate, and automate DJ's podcast conversation radar: global podcast discovery, episode ranking, daily text digests, and weekly audio briefings.", "tags": ["podcasts", "digest", "briefing", "discovery", "rss", "ranking", "audio-briefing"], "related_skills": ["podcast-audio-production", "youtube-content", "daily-business-briefing"], "content": "---\nname: podcast-intelligence-digest\ndescription: \"Build, run, calibrate, and automate DJ's podcast conversation radar: global podcast discovery, episode ranking, daily text digests, and weekly audio briefings.\"\nversion: 1.0.0\nauthor: Hermes Agent\nlicense: private\nmetadata:\n  hermes:\n    tags: [podcasts, digest, briefing, discovery, rss, ranking, audio-briefing]\n    related_skills: [podcast-audio-production, youtube-content, daily-business-briefing]\n---\n\n# Podcast Intelligence Digest\n\n## Overview\n\nUse this skill for DJ's \"global podcast conversation radar\": identifying the highest-signal podcast conversations from a broad source universe, ranking them against DJ's intellectual/professional taste, producing a concise daily text digest, and optionally turning the week's best conversations into a two-host audio digest.\n\nThe product is **not** a generic podcast newsletter. It should behave like a sharp analyst scanning the podcast world for conversations DJ would actually act on, listen to, or use to update a mental model.\n\n**Critical calibration rule:** score and evaluate **episodes**, not podcasts. Shows are source priors/discovery funnels only. A low-priority show can produce a must-listen episode; a high-priority show can produce a skip. Final recommendations must be episode-level.\n\n**Daily vs weekly distinction:** the daily text digest should cast a wider net and summarize what was said across more potentially relevant episodes. The weekly \"podcast of podcasts\" audio should be much more finely tuned: only the highest-signal, DJ-specific themes/conversations should make it into the script.\n\n## When to Use\n\nUse when the user asks to:\n\n- build, run, debug, or calibrate a podcast digest/radar/monitor;\n- collect podcast episodes from RSS feeds;\n- rank episodes by guest/topic relevance;\n- produce a daily podcast intelligence brief;\n- script or generate a weekly audio digest from podcast highlights;\n- expand discovery beyond subscribed shows by searching for high-signal guests.\n\nIf the task is purely converting an already-written script into audio, use `podcast-audio-production` instead. If podcast audio generation is part of a weekly digest pipeline, use this skill first for selection/scripting, then `podcast-audio-production` for production.\n\n## DJ Relevance Test\n\nEvery candidate episode must pass this question:\n\n> Will this conversation give DJ an action, framework, market read, operator insight, allocation implication, or mental-model shift?\n\nPrioritize:\n\n- market/business shapers;\n- top investors, allocators, and CEOs;\n- AI leaders and frontier thinkers;\n- rare-access conversations with decision-makers;\n- operator details from scaled companies;\n- durable frameworks, not news chatter;\n- business model, capital allocation, software economics, AI platform, labor/productivity, and company-building implications.\n\nPenalize or skip:\n\n- news recaps and politics-first commentary;\n- celebrity/entertainment interviews;\n- generic VC/promotional fluff;\n- second-tier partner promo episodes;\n- shallow founder origin stories without reusable ideas;\n- generic AI hype with no operator/investor specificity;\n- energy/climate/defense/geopolitics unless DJ explicitly redirects the product.\n\n## Source Model\n\nMaintain a three-ring source universe:\n\n1. **Ring 1 — calibration subscriptions**: shows DJ already values. Use these to understand taste and baseline signal.\n2. **Ring 2 — high-signal shows**: shows likely to host relevant CEOs, investors, AI leaders, and framework thinkers.\n3. **Ring 3 — global guest discovery**: do not rely only on known shows. Search podcast indexes/web for watchlist people appearing anywhere.\n\nKeep a guest/watchlist file as the strongest identity signal, but do not let a guest name in unrelated footer/link text dominate ranking.\n\n## Default Workflow\n\n1. **Load current configuration and prior outputs.**\n   - Inspect source lists, watchlists, skip rules, previous digest outputs, and the episode database when present.\n   - Do not assume a cron job exists or is approved; check first.\n\n2. **Resolve RSS feeds.**\n   - Use Apple/iTunes search and manual feed URLs.\n   - Record unresolved feeds explicitly rather than silently dropping them.\n   - Patch obvious wrong/broken feed entries as part of calibration.\n\n3. **Collect recent episodes.**\n   - Pull a configurable lookback window, usually 1 day for daily digest and 7 days for weekly/prototype runs.\n   - Store title, show, description, publication time, URL/audio URL, and source ring.\n\n4. **Metadata-rank broadly.**\n   - Score by guest/watchlist hits, show quality, title/description concepts, recency, and skip penalties.\n   - Treat metadata ranking as a first pass only. It is allowed to be crude; the final digest should not be.\n\n5. **Cluster duplicates.**\n   - Collapse the same interview appearing across multiple shows or feed variants.\n   - Recommend the best version first, and list alternates only when useful.\n\n6. **Extract content for finalists.**\n   - For top candidates, fetch transcripts if available, scrape episode pages, or summarize rich descriptions.\n   - Do not transcribe every episode by default; transcribe only finalists to control cost/time.\n\n7. **Curate manually/with LLM judgment.**\n   - Re-rank finalists using DJ's relevance test.\n   - Promote major CEO/operator/allocator conversations even when metadata keyword score is modest.\n   - Downgrade keyword-heavy generic AI/panel/news episodes.\n\n8. **W
```

```text
Got it — I processed your feedback and converted it into calibration rules.

## What I captured

You gave feedback on **65 episodes**:

- **Listen:** 7
- **Summarize:** 9
- **Interesting / maybe / learn more:** 12
- **Skip / no:** 36
- **Other:** 1

Saved human-readable calibration rules here:

`/opt/data/podcast_digest/calibration/2026-06-04_dj_feedback_rules.yaml`

## Main lesson

The Qwen filter was directionally useful but **too generous**, especially with `listen`.

Your actual preference is more like:

- **Listen:** scarce, original-audio-worthy
- **Summarize:** broad daily digest candidate
- **Scan:** maybe interesting, learn more first
- **Skip:** omit from digest unless calibration/debug

## Calibration examples now encoded

### Promote / listen-type signals

- Mercor CEO on AI labor economics / model-vs-app-layer defensibility
- Dara / Uber AV + marketplace strategy
- Gita Gopinath on global rates / AI capex inflation
- Ranjan Roy on AI boom warning signs
- Toast business breakdown
- Stratechery best-of content — always include/listen candidate

### Summarize, but don’t necessarily listen

- Fivetran / enterprise data infra for agents
- Axiom Math
- Operator-led PE
- RenMac market models
- Mark Pincus product frameworks
- Evolutionary AI models
- Personal AI workflows / second brain
- Terry Sejnowski
- China vs Nvidia

### Penalize / skip despite AI relevance

- Exa search infra promo
- Continual-learning technical research
- Video/generative-media agent demos
- Generic AI consciousness/alarmism
- Vertical vendor AI stories like Aircall/Mitel/elder care
- Corgi culture promo
- Elon/media narrative
- Consumer brand playbooks
- Crypto chatter
- Generic Bloomberg/news recaps

I patched:

`/opt/data/scripts/podcast_qwen_episode_score.py`

Then reran the 100-episode pass with the new calibration.

New output:

`/opt/data/podcast_digest/outputs/2026-06-04_2127-qwen-episode-calibration.md`

`/opt/data/podcast_digest/outputs/2026-06-04_2127-qwen-episode-scores.json`

## New run stats

- **Episodes scored:** 100
- **Token usage:** 42,402 total
- **Estimated cost:** ~$0.0298

Tier counts changed from:

- Before: listen=23, digest=37, scan=27, skip=13

To:

- After calibration: **listen=16, digest=41, scan=29, skip=14**

Still probably too generous, but better. The bigger win is that it moved some items you liked upward:

- Mercor moved to top 3
- Toast moved up strongly
- Gita Gopinath moved up strongly
- Vergecast Nvidia chip war moved up from scan to digest
- Ranjan Roy remained surfaced
- Stratechery best-of rule is now encoded

## Next step

Now I should stop producing calibration sheets and generate the first **actual calibrated daily digest** from this run:

- wider-net daily summary
- grouped by:
  - **Listen**
  - **Summarize**
  - **Interesting / learn more**
  - **Skipped noise**
- using your feedback as overrides where available
```

### user

```text
Review the conversation above and update the skill library. Be ACTIVE — most sessions produce at least one skill update, even if small. A pass that does nothing is a missed learning opportunity, not a neutral outcome.

Target shape of the library: CLASS-LEVEL skills, each with a rich SKILL.md and a `references/` directory for session-specific detail. Not a long flat list of narrow one-session-one-skill entries. This shapes HOW you update, not WHETHER you update.

Signals to look for (any one of these warrants action):
  • User corrected your style, tone, format, legibility, or verbosity. Frustration signals like 'stop doing X', 'this is too verbose', 'don't format like this', 'why are you explaining', 'just give me the answer', 'you always do Y and I hate it', or an explicit 'remember this' are FIRST-CLASS skill signals, not just memory signals. Update the relevant skill(s) to embed the preference so the next session starts already knowing.
  • User corrected your workflow, approach, or sequence of steps. Encode the correction as a pitfall or explicit step in the skill that governs that class of task.
  • Non-trivial technique, fix, workaround, debugging path, or tool-usage pattern emerged that a future session would benefit from. Capture it.
  • A skill that got loaded or consulted this session turned out to be wrong, missing a step, or outdated. Patch it NOW.

Preference order — prefer the earliest action that fits, but do pick one when a signal above fired:
  1. UPDATE A CURRENTLY-LOADED SKILL. Look back through the conversation for skills the user loaded via /skill-name or you read via skill_view. If any of them covers the territory of the new learning, PATCH that one first. It is the skill that was in play, so it's the right one to extend.
  2. UPDATE AN EXISTING UMBRELLA (via skills_list + skill_view). If no loaded skill fits but an existing class-level skill does, patch it. Add a subsection, a pitfall, or broaden a trigger.
  3. ADD A SUPPORT FILE under an existing umbrella. Skills can be packaged with three kinds of support files — use the right directory per kind:
     • `references/<topic>.md` — session-specific detail (error transcripts, reproduction recipes, provider quirks) AND condensed knowledge banks: quoted research, API docs, external authoritative excerpts, or domain notes you found while working on the problem. Write it concise and for the value of the task, not as a full mirror of upstream docs.
     • `templates/<name>.<ext>` — starter files meant to be copied and modified (boilerplate configs, scaffolding, a known-good example the agent can `reproduce with modifications`).
     • `scripts/<name>.<ext>` — statically re-runnable actions the skill can invoke directly (verification scripts, fixture generators, deterministic probes, anything the agent should run rather than hand-type each time).
     Add support files via skill_manage action=write_file with file_path starting 'references/', 'templates/', or 'scripts/'. The umbrella's SKILL.md should gain a one-line pointer to any new support file so future agents know it exists.
  4. CREATE A NEW CLASS-LEVEL UMBRELLA SKILL when no existing skill covers the class. The name MUST be at the class level. The name MUST NOT be a specific PR number, error string, feature codename, library-alone name, or 'fix-X / debug-Y / audit-Z-today' session artifact. If the proposed name only makes sense for today's task, it's wrong — fall back to (1), (2), or (3).

User-preference embedding (important): when the user expressed a style/format/workflow preference, the update belongs in the SKILL.md body, not just in memory. Memory captures 'who the user is and what the current situation and state of your operations are'; skills capture 'how to do this class of task for this user'. When they complain about how you handled a task, the skill that governs that task needs to carry the lesson.

If you notice two existing skills that overlap, note it in your reply — the background curator handles consolidation at scale.

Do NOT capture (these become persistent self-imposed constraints that bite you later when the environment changes):
  • Environment-dependent failures: missing binaries, fresh-install errors, post-migration path mismatches, 'command not found', unconfigured credentials, uninstalled packages. The user can fix these — they are not durable rules.
  • Negative claims about tools or features ('browser tools do not work', 'X tool is broken', 'cannot use Y from execute_code'). These harden into refusals the agent cites against itself for months after the actual problem was fixed.
  • Session-specific transient errors that resolved before the conversation ended. If retrying worked, the lesson is the retry pattern, not the original failure.
  • One-off task narratives. A user asking 'summarize today's market' or 'analyze this PR' is not a class of work that warrants a skill.

If a tool failed because of setup state, capture the FIX (install command, config step, env var to set) under an existing setup or troubleshooting skill — never 'this tool does not work' as a standalone constraint.

'Nothing to save.' is a real option but should NOT be the default. If the session ran smoothly with no corrections and produced no new technique, just say 'Nothing to save.' and stop. Otherwise, act.
```

Before telling DJ the key is missing, check the gateway process env:\n\n``​`python\nfrom pathlib import Path\nkey = None\nfor item in Path('/proc/1/environ').read_bytes().split(b'\\0'):\n    if item.startswith(b'OPENROUTER_API_KEY=REDACTED        key = item.split(b'=', 1)[1].decode()\n        break\nprint(bool(key))\n``​`\n\nDo not print the key. {"success": true, "name": "podcast-intelligence-digest", "file": "scripts/qwen_episode_score.py", "content": "#!/usr/bin/env python3\n\"\"\"Reusable starter for compact OpenRouter/Qwen podcast episode scoring.\n\nAssumes an episodes SQLite table with columns similar to DJ's prototype:\nid, show_name, ring, priority, title, link, published, summary, duration.\nAdapt DB/OUTDIR paths as needed.\n\"\"\"\nimport html\nimport json\nimport os\nimport re\nimport sqlite3\nimport time\nfrom pathlib import Path\nfrom datetime import datetime, timezone\n\nimport requests\n\nBASE = Path(os.getenv(\"PODCAST_DIGEST_DIR\", \"/opt/data/podcast_digest\"))\nDB = BASE / \"episodes.sqlite\"\nOUTDIR = BASE / \"outputs\"\nMODEL = os.getenv(\"PODCAST_OSS_MODEL\", \"qwen/qwen3-235b-a22b\")\nBATCH_SIZE = int(os.getenv(\"PODCAST_SCORE_BATCH_SIZE\", \"10\"))\n\n\ndef get_openrouter_key():\n    val = os.getenv(\"OPENROUTER_API_KEY\")\n    if val:\n        return val\n    # Railway gateway quirk: terminal subprocess env can be stale after redeploy.\n    try:\n        for item in Path(\"/proc/1/environ\").read_bytes().split(b\"\\0\"):\n            if item.startswith(b\"OPENROUTER_API_KEY=REDACTED                return item.split(b\"=\", 1)[1].decode()\n    except Exception:\n        pass\n    return None\n\n\ndef clean(text, limit=1000):\n    if not text:\n        return \"\"\n    text = re.sub(r\"<[^>]+>\", \" \", str(text))\n    text = html.unescape(text)\n    text = re.sub(r\"\\s+\", \" \", text).strip()\n    return text[:limit]\n\n\ndef load_episodes():\n    con = sqlite3.connect(DB)\n    con.row_factory = sqlite3.Row\n    return [dict(r) for r in con.execute(\"\"\"\n        select id, show_name, ring, priority, title, link, published, summary, duration\n        from episodes\n        order by published desc, show_name, title\n    \"\"\")]\n\n\ndef score_batch(api_key, batch):\n    system = \"\"\"You are DJ Mauch's podcast episode filter. Score EPISODES, not shows.\nReturn ONLY valid JSON: {\"results\":[{\"id\":\"...\",\"score\":0-100,\"tier\":\"skip|scan|digest|listen\",\"reason\":\"<=24 words\",\"confidence\":\"low|medium|high\"}]}.\nDaily text digest should cast a wide net; weekly audio should be selective. Favor market reads, top investors/CEOs, AI platform/business-model insight, durable frameworks, and operator insight. Penalize generic AI hype, news recaps, politics, celebrity, generic VC fluff, and shallow promo.\"\"\"\n    payload = [{\n        \"id\": e[\"id\"],\n        \"show\": e.get(\"show_name\"),\n        \"ring\": e.get(\"ring\"),\n        \"source_priority\": e.get(\"priority\"),\n        \"title\": clean(e.get(\"title\"), 300),\n        \"published\": e.get(\"published\"),\n        \"duration_seconds\": e.get(\"duration\"),\n        \"summary\": clean(e.get(\"summary\"), 1000),\n    } for e in batch]\n    user = \"/no_think\\nScore these podcast episodes. Return only JSON.\\n\" + json.dumps(payload, ensure_ascii=False)\n    resp = requests.post(\n        \"https://openrouter.ai/api/v1/chat/completions\",\n        headers={\n            \"Authorization\": f\"Bearer {api_key}\",\n            \"Content-Type\": \"application/json\",\n            \"HTTP-Referer\": \"https://hermes-agent.local/podcast-digest\",\n            \"X-Title\": \"Hermes Podcast Intelligence\",\n        },\n        json={\n            \"model\": MODEL,\n            \"messages\": [{\"role\": \"system\", \"content\": system}, {\"role\": \"user\", \"content\": user}],\n            \"temperature\": 0.1,\n            \"max_tokens\": 1800,\n        },\n        timeout=120,\n    )\n    resp.raise_for_status()\n    data = resp.json()\n    content = data[\"choices\"][0][\"message\"][\"content\"]\n    parsed = json.loads(re.search(r\"\\{.*\\}\", content, re.S).group(0))\n    return parsed.get(\"results\", []), data.get(\"usage\", {})\n\n\ndef main():\n    api_key = REDACTED    if not api_key:REDACTED        raise SystemExit(\"OPENROUTER_API_KEY not found\")\n    episodes = load_episodes()\n    scored = []\n    usage_total = {\"prompt_tokens\": 0, \"completion_tokens\": 0, \"total_tokens\": 0}\n    for i in range(0, len(episodes), BATCH_SIZE):\n        batch = episodes[i:i+BATCH_SIZE]\n        results, usage = score_batch(api_key, batch)\n        by_id = {str(r.get(\"id\")): r for r in results}\n        for e in batch:\n            scored.append({**e, \"qwen\": by_id.get(e[\"id\"], {\"score\": 0, \"tier\": \"scan\", \"reason\": \"Model omitted this episode.\", \"confidence\": \"low\"})})\n        for k in usage_total:\n            usage_total[k] += int(usage.get(k, 0) or 0)\n        print(f\"scored {min(i+BATCH_SIZE, len(episodes))}/{len(episodes)}\")\n        time.sleep(0.5)\n    OUTDIR.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)\n    stamp = datetime.now(timezone.utc).strftime(\"%Y-%m-%d_%H%M\")\n    out = OUTDIR / f\"{stamp}-qwen-episode-scores.json\"\n    out.write_text(json.dumps({\"model\": MODEL, \"usage\": usage_total, \"episodes\": scored}, indent=2, ensure_ascii=False))\n    print(out)\n\n\nif __name__ == \"__main__\":\n    main()\n", "file_type": ".py"}
